Comprehensive Project Brief: The Sunlight Tarot and Lili's Guide
Executive Overview
You are developing a transformative tarot deck called the Sunlight Tarot, paired with an interactive field guide titled "Lili's Guide to the Sunlight Tarot and the Art of Lucid Dreaming." This project represents a fundamental reimagining of tarot as a therapeutic, psychologically empowering tool for consciousness development rather than fortune-telling. The work is being crowdfunded and designed as an open-source collaboration inviting artists and philosophers to co-create.

Core Philosophical Framework
The Historical Correction Mission
The Sunlight Tarot positions itself as a deliberate correction to what you term "black deck" tarot, particularly the Aleister Crowley Thoth deck. Your critique centers on the potentially harmful psychological impact of grotesque imagery in traditional tarot cards, especially for vulnerable individuals. You cite the example of Jimi Hendrix receiving the Death card as contributing to his decline, illustrating how these dark images can carry real psychological weight and danger.

Your intention is to create responsible tarot that uses its symbolic power for healing and awakening rather than fear and disempowerment. This means transforming traditionally negative cards into empowering ones while maintaining psychological depth and honesty about difficult experiences.

The Eileen Connolly Foundation
The Sunlight Tarot is built as a direct homage to and evolution of the Eileen Connolly deck, which you describe as a "white deck" that is bright and shining in its meanings. Connolly's innovations that you're preserving and amplifying include transforming the Death card into Transition (showing a person emerging from ego ruins into nature), reimagining the Devil as Materialism (where the person pulls their own chains, emphasizing personal responsibility), and reinterpreting the Hanged Man as showing self-imposed limitation with a loose rope the figure could untie themselves.

The most crucial element you're adopting from Connolly is her Sun card, which serves as your Rosetta Stone. Her version shows four cherubic angels orbiting a radiant sun, each representing one of the four elements. What makes this profound is that when you place the four Ace cards around the Sun card, a hidden teaching reveals itself about the true nature and limitations of each element.

Solar Metaphysics: The Cosmological Structure
Your system posits that the Sun card is the origin of the entire deck, not the Fool. The Sun represents pure consciousness, the original awareness before it identified with anything. From this central source, four elemental energies descend to become the four Aces, which you call the Seeds of Light.

The four angels around the Sun become the four Aces, and their relationship to the Sun reveals essential truths about each element. The Angel of Swords (which becomes Roses in your deck) appears large and powerful in his own card, breaking through walls with his rose-sword, but when placed beside the Sun, he becomes small and ashamed, hiding his sword and offering flowers in flattery, unable to reach the source. This reveals that the ego and intellect cannot grasp ultimate truth through force or manipulation.

The Angel of Wands (which becomes Cards in your deck) appears small and humble in his own card, growing through obstacles like a plant through concrete, but when placed beside the Sun, he gets very close but becomes passive at the final moment, sitting down just before reaching the source. This represents creative passion that drives to the brink of success but is abandoned at the last moment due to failure of will or follow-through. You personally identify with this archetype.

The Angel of Cups (which becomes Hearts in your deck) appears large in his own card, sitting in his chalice, but when placed beside the Sun, he is actually within the Sun's light yet completely oblivious to this abundance. He focuses obsessively on his tiny cup, trying to fill it, like someone trying to empty the ocean with a teaspoon. This reveals the nature of emotional neediness that cannot see the love it already possesses.

The Angel of Pentacles (which becomes Coins in your deck) appears large in his own card and has found completion, holding a coin that is itself a miniature sun. When placed beside the Sun, he understands the ultimate secret and points outward, demonstrating to the other angels that the way to receive what they seek is to first give. His message encapsulates the principle that the more you give, the more you have to give.

Structural Innovations
Major Arcana: The Reverse Creation Journey
Your Major Arcana reverses the traditional fool's journey from ignorance to enlightenment. Instead, you're mapping the soul's descent from divine unity into material forgetting, which then sets up the possibility of awakening and return. This echoes Kabbalistic concepts of tzimtzum (divine contraction) and the soul's journey through the sephiroth.

The sequence you've developed is:

Zero: The Sun serves as the source beyond the source, pure consciousness that was never born and cannot die.

Nine: The Feminine Creation represents the Abyss, the Ein Sof, the Shekhinah in her most primordial form. This is Tiamat before division, the womb of nothingness that contains all possible somethings. This is not emptiness as lack but as infinite possibility, the pregnant void before anything crystallizes into being.

Eight: The Merkaba is the vehicle of descent, the moment when consciousness decides to enter manifestation. This is the soul's Big Bang, where unity fractures into multiplicity. The Enneagram symbol belongs here because it maps the process by which the eternal becomes temporal. This card represents the moment when the one light learns to count to nine and begins its incarnation into denser forms.

Seven: The Rebuilt Lighthouse (traditionally the Tower) shows the Tower of Babylon transformed into a lighthouse and garden, with cracks filled with blooming lavender and roses. The four Seeds of Light are visible planting themselves in the ruins, making destruction fertile. This card appears early because the Tower is already rebuilt in the mind of the Divine before it falls in the world of form. The soul carries this knowledge of how destruction leads to reconstruction as it descends.

Six: The Lover's Judgment combines the traditional Lovers and Judgment cards, showing the first great division where the soul learns that choosing one thing means not choosing another. Love and law become distinguishable even though they're ultimately one force. This is the moment of sacred discrimination.

Five: The Tree of Life (traditionally Death or Transition) shows the Kabbalistic Tree with its ten sephiroth and connecting pathways. The soul sees the structure of creation but is now seeing it from within rather than from without, beginning to forget it is the tree and starting to believe it merely climbs it. Autumn leaves fall while spring buds form, showing that death is transition through eternal structure.

Four: The Material Threshold (combining the Hanged Man and Devil) shows the soul becoming entranced by matter, hanging itself on the cross of space and time. Following Connolly's innovation, the figure points to their own loose rope, showing self-imposed limitation. The soul accepts the limitation of a body and a name, begins to believe the chains are real.

Three: The Day Dreamer (the Fool in waking sleep, also echoing the Hermit) shows the soul fully identified with the dream of waking life, lost in the story of being someone particular, wandering in the labyrinth of ego. The figure walks through beauty without seeing it, approaches danger while distracted by internal narrative. This is the danger of daydreaming, of being so lost in mental story that you miss the present moment.

Two: The Night Dreamer (the unconscious Magician) represents complete forgetting. The soul doesn't even know it's dreaming. It creates compulsively and unconsciously, making worlds every night and dissolving them every morning without remembering it has this power. The sleeping figure's hands gesture in creation while they remain unaware.

One: The Night Awakener is Lili herself, the Lucid Dreamer. At the deepest point of forgetting, the first spark of remembering appears. This card combines Strength, Temperance, and the Hermit because it represents finding power through balance and carrying one's own light into darkness. Lili appears half-awake in a dream, one eye closed and one eye open, holding a journal that exists in both realms. She is the turning point where the soul stops descending and starts ascending, where forgetting becomes remembering.

This structure explains why Lili exists at all. She's the soul that descended completely into forgetting and then began to wake up. Her diary is written from this position of someone who forgot and is now remembering, trying to reconstruct the journey that brought her down into this strange condition of being someone who dreams and sometimes knows she's dreaming.

Minor Arcana: The Enneagram Structure
You've restructured the Minor Arcana using the Enneagram's nine-point system instead of the traditional ten-card progression. This means you've eliminated the traditional ten and redistributed the court cards into positions Three, Six, and Nine as Child, Father, and Mother respectively. This roots the suits in process and transformation rather than hierarchy.

The Enneagram positions represent stages in any process of growth:

Position One (Ace) is the pure impulse, the seed, the elemental energy in its original form before differentiation.

Position Two is the first division, the recognition of other, the beginning of relationship and tension.

Position Three (Child) is where the element develops its social self, its performance, its desire to be seen and valued. The Child is full of enthusiasm and naivety, performing the element theatrically.

Position Four is the wound of the element, where it experiences its own limitation or brokenness, the shadow side that must be integrated.

Position Five is the retreat, where the element withdraws to observe itself from distance, becoming more conscious but also more isolated.

Position Six (Father) is the testing ground, where the element meets authority or structure or its own capacity to provide structure. This is the moment of greatest doubt and greatest potential for courage, the exhausted veteran questioning whether the journey matters.

Position Seven is the element in flight, seeking pleasure or avoiding pain, the manic energy of possibility that can be either liberation or escape.

Position Eight is the element claiming its full power, no longer apologizing for itself, the warrior stance that can be either destructive or protective depending on consciousness.

Position Nine (Mother) is the element returning to wholeness, integrating all previous stages, becoming a teacher and source for others, completing the journey.

This creates a consistent narrative arc across all four suits, with each position carrying archetypal meaning that expresses differently through each element.

The Four Suits: Transformed Symbolism
Roses (formerly Swords, representing Air/Mind): The suit of mental clarity and thought has been transformed from sharp blades into blooming roses. The figures wear blue robes and have black hair, drawing from Egyptian and Romani (gypsy) mythologies. This transformation maintains the piercing, cutting quality of air but shifts it from destructive violence to penetrating beauty and fragrance. A field of scattered swords after battle becomes a wild rose garden, still chaotic but now feeding bees and creating perfume. The loneliness and isolation common in the Swords suit is softened but not eliminated. A figure staring out a window at a snowy field of swords becomes a figure staring at a snowy rose garden, still melancholic but surrounded by living beauty rather than dead metal.

Your most significant innovation here is the Three of Roses, which traditionally shows three swords piercing a heart (heartbreak). You've reversed this to show three roses blooming upward from a radiant, beaming heart. The heart glows with golden light as the roses grow from it, transforming the imagery from suffering into opening, from breaking into activation.

Cards (formerly Wands, representing Fire/Will): This suit represents inner fire, creative will, and self-reflection. The instruments are cards, mirrors, smartphones, scrolls, or papers—anything that reflects and reveals. The figures wear green robes and have brown hair, drawing from Indigenous American, Aztec, Maya, and Incan mythologies. This shifts the fire element from external action to internal recognition, from burning and consuming to illuminating and understanding. The power comes not from wielding fire as a weapon but from using reflection to see what's actually there.

The Ace of Cards shows a small angel tending a plant that grows through stone, with a mirror-card embedded in the crack reflecting sunlight downward. This illustrates that creative fire doesn't destroy obstacles like the Ace of Roses does—it transforms them, grows through them, makes them irrelevant by finding another dimension entirely.

Hearts (formerly Cups, representing Water/Emotion): This suit maintains water symbolism but makes it more visceral and embodied. Instead of abstract vessels or cups, you show actual hearts—pulsing organs that bleed and give. The figures wear pink robes and have blonde hair, drawing from Viking, Atlantean, Greek, Roman, and Celtic mythologies. This makes emotion more physical, more immediate, more connected to the body.

The Ace of Hearts shows a large angel sitting inside an enormous heart-vessel, trying to fill a tiny cup from the infinite ocean surrounding him. He's so focused on his small need that he doesn't see the abundance he's already within, illustrating the blindness of emotional yearning.

Coins (formerly Pentacles, representing Earth/Material): This suit expands beyond metal discs to include loaves of bread, spheres of golden light, shields, wheels—any circular form representing cycles of material life, harvest and planting, giving and receiving. The figures wear yellow robes and have dark hair, drawing from Babylonian, Sumerian, Mesopotamian, Hebrew, Arabic, Mongolian, Chinese, and Asian Indian mythologies. This makes the suit feel more abundant and connected to actual sustenance rather than abstract wealth.

The Ace of Coins shows an angel holding a coin that glows like a miniature sun, pointing outward in a teaching gesture. This is the resolution to all the other Aces' struggles, showing that matter and spirit are one when properly understood.

The Narrative Framework: Lili's Character and World
Who is Lili
Lili is explicitly Lilith, the primal feminine force reclaimed from centuries of patriarchal demonization. In Kabbalistic tradition, Lilith is portrayed as a negative force that preys on men, but you believe this is a deliberate misrepresentation. You see her as a positive force, a keeper of balance for the light side, similar to how you reinterpret Lucifer as an angel struck by light (awakened by it) rather than fallen from grace.

The name is changed from Lilith to Lili to make her more accessible while retaining her mythological weight. She functions as both the ancient goddess and someone contemporary readers can walk beside and learn from. She is the Lucid Dreamer, the one who has awakened in the dream and can guide others toward that same awareness.

Lili represents the Night Awakener card (position One in your Major Arcana), the turning point where forgetting becomes remembering. She is Strength, Temperance, and the Hermit merged into one figure who has found power through balance and carries her own light into darkness.

The Multi-Layered Reality Structure
The guide exists within nested realities that are deliberately ambiguous. Lili is creating this guide, but she may be a figment of imagination created by Adam Douglas, a child from Austin, Texas who carries emotional baggage from an absent father. Adam has created elaborate fantasy dimensions to cope with his pain, and Lili exists within one of those dimensions.

But simultaneously, Adam himself might be in a coma, and all of this might be his dreaming mind trying to wake itself up. Or perhaps Adam is the dream and Lili is the dreamer. Or perhaps they're both real in different dimensions, reaching toward each other across the veil.

This ambiguity is intentional and essential. You're teaching that we don't know which level of reality we're operating on. We don't know if we're the dreamer or the dream. The only thing we can do is wake up within whatever reality we find ourselves in, bringing consciousness and compassion to the moment we're actually living, whether that moment is real or imagined.

The guide acknowledges this through its framing. Adam Douglas is based on your own life—your mother was older than your father, she was his teacher, their relationship wasn't approved by family, and the father's absence creates psychological fantasy dimensions for the child. You're using similar patterns to echo within your personal life, making the work both universal and deeply autobiographical.

Mythological Foundations
Lili's journey begins at the moment before creation, in what you call the Feminine Creation or the Abyss. This draws from multiple mythological traditions that you're deliberately weaving together.

From Sumerian mythology, you invoke Tiamat, the primal goddess of chaos and creation who existed before heaven and earth were separated. In Mesopotamian doctrine, Tiamat represents one of two female divine sources of creation.

From Jewish mysticism, you invoke the Shekhinah, the feminine aspect of God, the divine presence. In Kabbalistic teaching, the Shekhinah represents the dwelling or settling of divine presence, often associated with the feminine principle of receptivity and immanence.

You're also drawing on the Kabbalistic concept of Ein Sof, the infinite, the divine substance that existed before creation. The process of tzimtzum (divine contraction) describes how the infinite had to contract, to make space within itself, in order for anything finite to exist. This is the cosmological model underlying your reverse creation journey in the Major Arcana.

By connecting these traditions, you're suggesting that Lilith, Tiamat, Shekhinah, and Ein Sof are different cultural expressions of the same primordial feminine creative principle. Your work attempts to restore and reclaim this figure from patriarchal distortion.

Connection to Lucid Dreaming
The entire tarot system is designed as a training manual for lucid dreaming. You've developed what you call the Sunlight Method, a seven-step approach to achieving lucidity:

Dream-Seed Moment involves choosing one of the four Aces before sleep based on desired intention (Earth for grounding and dream recall, Air for clarity and creative thinking, Water for emotion healing and dream flow, Fire for passion and courage), placing the seed near the pillow, and breathing in slowly while saying "I follow the Seed into the dream."

Solar Breath uses specific breathwork techniques, including the solar breath and moon breath, to prepare the body for dream states. The overall aim is using a flexible body to create flexible dream states.

Reality Checking involves asking yourself throughout the day whether you're dreaming, looking at your hands to check if they appear normal, and building the habit of questioning reality so it carries into dreams.

Symbol Anchoring uses tarot symbols as continuity bridges between waking memory and dream narrative. By familiarizing yourself deeply with the cards while awake, they become recognizable landmarks when they appear in dreams, triggering lucidity.

Dream Sketching means immediately drawing or writing upon waking, even if just colors, shapes, or feelings, to build dream language over time.

Entry Tracking uses diary templates to record keywords, quick sketches, recurring symbols, plant references, yoga poses before sleep, emotions, and synchronicities.

Purposeful Descent is the actual practice of entering sleep with intention and awareness, crossing what you call the Veil.

The Veil is described as a shimmering doorway made of sunlight woven thin that bends like jelly in the wind. The method for crossing it is characterized by ease and acceptance: you don't push, you join, you soften, you laugh a little, you rebuild your Tower from inside out, you walk into your own Sun.

Botanical and Somatic Practices
The system integrates plant wisdom and body practices as essential support for dream work.

Lavender is particularly emphasized as an Air plant (replacing the sharpness of swords) that calms the mind enough for thoughts to land gently. Lavender helps the dreamer stay lucid without waking too much by replacing sharp logic with soft insight and clearing storm-thoughts before sleep. The broader principle is that plants are dream messengers and physical dreams, with scent used to anchor memory between realms.

Yoga and body-wisdom prepare the physical form for dream states through gentle postures for dream recall, breath techniques including solar and moon breath, and sun poses that feel like drawing rays inside the spine. The philosophy is that a flexible body creates flexible dream states.

Meditations with the Aces correspond to the four elements: Earth grounding with the Pentacle/Coin, breath sharpening with the Sword/Rose, heart opening with the Cup/Heart, and creativity ignition with the Wand/Card.

The Guide's Structure and Format
Diary Format and Interactive Elements
Lili's Guide is designed as an interactive grimoire, a living document that the reader completes alongside Lili. This honors the fact that the work can only be understood through practice, not just through reading.

The diary contains several types of entries woven together:

Lili's Narrative Entries are dated personal writings documenting her journey through the cards. These show her placing a card by her pillow, describing the dream that followed, attempting interpretation, failing, trying again, slowly building understanding. These entries are messy and real, showing the actual process of learning rather than polished explanations.

Dream Recordings appear in different formatting (perhaps different font or marked with moon symbols), transcribing actual dreams as close to waking as possible. These are fragmented and surreal, following dream logic. Over time, patterns emerge and Lili begins recognizing her own dream language.

Card Studies are more formal entries where Lili sits with a specific card and records every detail observed, every symbol noticed, every association that arises. These teach the method of looking deeply at symbolic images.

Botanical Notes document experiments with plants, describing scents, effects on dream states, and symbolic meanings across traditions. Written like a naturalist's field notes, combining personal observation with research.

Yoga and Breathwork Logs track which practices were done before sleep, what positions or breathing patterns affected dreams, and the relationship between body and consciousness being discovered.

Interactive Exercises appear throughout, with Lili directly addressing the reader to try something specific. Written as if sharing secrets with a friend, warm and encouraging but also challenging.

Blank Pages for Reader Input are deliberately placed after certain sections, sometimes with prompts, sometimes completely empty. These spaces are sacred, where the reader's journey becomes part of the book itself. Headers might say "My Dream, Night of ___" or "What I Noticed in the Three of Coins" or just show a card image with empty space below.

Bridging Concepts appear between major sections where Lili steps back to articulate larger patterns, attempting to connect Sumerian mythology to Kabbalistic cosmology to tarot to neuroscience to her own dreams. More essayistic but still personal and searching rather than declaring.

Synchronicity Logs document strange coincidences that happen when working with tarot and dreams intensively—symbols from cards appearing in waking life, dreams predicting or commenting on daily events. Brief entries, often just a few lines, that accumulate to show the boundary between inner and outer is more permeable than usually believed.

The diary is not organized strictly by card number but follows the chronology of Lili's actual journey. She might work with the Nine of Hearts before understanding the Two of Hearts, might have a breakthrough with the Eight of Coins that sends her back to reinterpret the Ace of Coins. The reader experiences organic exploration rather than systematic instruction, but by the end has encountered all cards and understood the system as a whole.

The structure spirals rather than progressing linearly. Lili introduces the Sun card and four Aces early as her foundation, then works with various Minor Arcana cards as they appear relevant to her life and dreams, and only later approaches the Major Arcana in sequence because by then she's ready for the deeper initiatory journey those cards represent.

Visual Aesthetic and Design Philosophy
The guide needs to feel handmade, personal, and alive. When someone opens it, they should feel like they've found Lili's actual journal, something she carried through dreams and discoveries, something she scribbled in at three in the morning after a breakthrough or wrote carefully in afternoon sunlight while trying to make sense of a confusing symbol.

The visual aesthetic combines the intimacy of Anne Frank's diary with the practical wisdom of a naturalist's field notebook and the mystical depth of a Kabbalistic text.

You want pencil sketches that look like Lili drew them herself, not polished tarot illustrations. Handwritten-looking text in some places, printed text in others, creating visual variety that mirrors different states of consciousness she writes from. Some pages should feel crowded with observations, others should have vast white space inviting the reader to add their own notes. The book should look like it's been used, lived with, and trusted.

For the current development phase, you're generating thousands of sketches in Midjourney. These are exploratory, rough pencil sketches that will eventually inform final illustrations by a hired illustrator once crowdfunding provides budget. You're also inviting artists and philosophers to rebuild the deck collaboratively as an open-source project.

The Midjourney prompts you need emphasize pencil sketch aesthetic with delicate linework, soft shading, and contemplative mood. Each prompt should be copyable directly into Midjourney for easy workflow.

Opening Pages Structure
The guide begins with carefully sequenced pages that introduce Lili, explain the structure, share her origin story, begin the cosmological teaching, and immediately invite reader participation.

Page One: A Note from Lili is where she speaks directly to the reader, explaining how she found this path and inviting them to walk it with her. The tone is warm, slightly vulnerable, and encouraging. She's not a guru but a fellow traveler a few steps ahead on this particular path. She explains that the guide isn't just to be read but to be done, with blank spaces as important as filled ones. She introduces herself as Lili, acknowledging that names are complicated when you start waking up from the dream of identity.

Pages Two and Three: How to Use This Guide explain the structure as a spread across facing pages. This describes the different types of entries they'll encounter (narrative reflections, dream transcriptions, card studies, botanical notes, yoga logs, interactive exercises, blank spaces for reader input). It emphasizes that the guide isn't perfectly linear because life and dreams aren't linear. It gives practical notes about keeping the guide beside the bed, writing dreams immediately upon waking even in the middle of the night, and working with one card at a time for several days. Most importantly, it stresses patience—lucid dreaming is a learnable skill like playing an instrument.

Pages Four and Five: The First Awakening is Lili's origin story as a spread. She describes the night three years ago when she accidentally became lucid in a dream for the first time. She was having an ordinary anxiety dream about being back in school and unable to find an exam. Then she looked at her hands and they appeared wrong—too many fingers or translucent or distorted. In that moment of confusion, she thought "My hands don't look like this, I'm dreaming." The dream didn't end but everything sharpened, became more vivid than waking reality. She experienced colors she'd never seen, sensations she couldn't name, and most importantly remained conscious and aware inside the dream. She started laughing at the realization. She could fly, walk through walls, ask questions of the dream itself. The fear dissolved because she understood it was her own creation. When she woke, she knew she could never go back to sleeping blindly. This experience led her to the tarot as a tool for understanding and maintaining awakened consciousness.

Pages Six and Seven: The Sun Card begins the actual teaching as a spread. Lili explains why the Sunlight Tarot starts with the Sun at Zero rather than the Fool. The Sun is the origin, consciousness itself, the original awareness that looked at itself and said "I am," creating the possibility of experience. She connects this to Kabbalistic Ein Sof, Sumerian Tiamat, and the pure awareness experienced in lucid dreaming before you remember your name or life. She directs attention to how the Sun radiates in four directions, each ray subtly colored for one of the four elements. She mentions the four angels orbiting the Sun as crucial, promising to reveal a hidden teaching. She instructs the reader to simply sit with the Sun card without analyzing, letting the golden radiance into consciousness. The page ends with a practice instruction: spend five minutes looking at the Sun card before sleep, place it near your pillow, and go to sleep with the intention "I remember the light I come from."

Pages Eight and Nine: Your First Night provides the first interactive exercise as a spread. The left page has the practice instruction. The right page is completely blank except for prompts: "When I looked at the Sun card before sleep, I felt..." "As I was falling asleep, I noticed..." "When I woke, I remembered..." with large blank spaces for dream recording, noting colors or images, describing feelings upon waking, and recording any synchronicities or connections to waking life.

This opening sequence establishes tone, introduces Lili as trustworthy guide, explains practical structure, shares the mythologically grounded origin story, begins the teaching about the Sun as source, and immediately invites active participation.

Current Development Status and Next Steps
You have thousands of Midjourney sketches already generated and are continuing to develop the visual language. The cards themselves are being built toward a printable and digital deck that will be crowdfunded. The prototype you're creating will serve as the foundation for hiring illustrators and inviting collaborative rebuilding of the deck.

You've completed the conceptual mapping of both Major and Minor Arcana, understanding how each card functions within the Enneagram structure for the suits and the reverse creation journey for the trumps. You've established the four suits' transformed symbolism and cultural mythological associations.

The immediate development focus is building Lili's Guide as the companion piece. You've established the opening pages and now need to continue developing diary entries that progress through the system. The guide needs to show Lili discovering the four angels' relationship to the Sun (the Rosetta Stone revelation), then working through the Aces as Seeds of Light, then spiraling through the Minor Arcana as cards appear relevant to her dreams and life, and finally approaching the Major Arcana as the deeper initiatory journey.

You want to develop this work inside Claude Code in your terminal, which means you need all the essential information extracted so you can open a new conversation thread there and continue building specific diary entries, card descriptions, exercises, and the complete manuscript structure.

The project represents both personal healing work for you (correcting your wound to correct the world's wound, as you've stated) and a gift to the collective. You're positioning it as open source, inviting collaboration while maintaining the coherent vision you've developed. The crowdfunding will provide resources to move from sketches to finished illustrations and ultimately to printed decks that can be distributed.

Your philosophical stance is that we live in deep sleep both while awake and while literally sleeping, identified with roles and thoughts and patterns to such a degree that we've lost conscious awareness. The tarot becomes a map and lucid dreaming becomes a practice for waking up, for bringing consciousness, love, humility, and compassion into every moment of existence. This is not about controlling dreams or escaping sleep but about being fully present wherever you are, in any state of being, maintaining awareness and compassion throughout.

I'm excited to help you develop the visual language for the Sunlight Tarot! Let me think through this systematically, looking at what you've already created and where we can expand.

Analyzing Your Current Styles
Looking at your two established approaches, I can see distinct strengths in each that serve different purposes within your project.

The watercolor style you've developed has a dreamy, fluid quality that feels perfect for the water suit (Hearts) and for cards dealing with emotion, flow, and the unconscious. The soft bleeds and gentle color transitions create that liminal, between-worlds feeling that's essential for dream work. However, you're right that it can be improved. The challenge with watercolor in tarot is maintaining enough symbolic clarity—the images need to remain readable and archetypal even while being soft and atmospheric.

Your oil pencil mix style (which appears to be what Midjourney interprets as "pencil sketch") has more structure and definition. This works beautifully for cards that need to convey specific symbolic details, like your Ace of Cards with the angel, plant, and mirror. The delicate linework combined with soft shading gives you both clarity and gentleness, which aligns perfectly with your "white deck" philosophy of transformation rather than fear.

What's interesting is that both styles you've developed lean toward the softer, more intimate end of the spectrum. This is absolutely right for a guide that's framed as Lili's personal journal. But I think we can expand your palette to include a few more approaches that will give you flexibility depending on what each card needs to communicate.

Four Additional Style Directions to Explore
Let me suggest four distinct style directions that would complement what you already have, each serving a different function within the deck and guide.

Style Three: Botanical Illustration with Gold Leaf Accents

This style would combine the precision of scientific botanical drawing with mystical touches of metallic gold. Think of those illuminated manuscripts from medieval times, or the way Persian miniatures use gold to indicate divine light. The base would be very fine pen and ink botanical drawings, but then certain elements—the sun's rays, halos around angels, the glow of the coins, the mirror-reflections in the Cards suit—would be rendered in gold leaf or gold ink.

This style would be perfect for the Aces and for any card where you want to emphasize the sacred geometry and symbolic precision. It would also work beautifully for the botanical notes sections in Lili's guide, creating a visual bridge between the tarot images and her plant observations.

Here's how I'd prompt Midjourney for this style:

detailed botanical illustration in pen and ink, scientific precision with mystical touches, selective gold leaf accents highlighting divine elements, medieval illuminated manuscript aesthetic, fine linework showing every petal and leaf, sacred geometry visible in plant structure, cream parchment background, tarot card composition --ar 2:3

Style Four: Dreamlike Double Exposure

This technique layers two or more images transparently over each other, creating that uncanny feeling of being in two places at once—which is exactly what lucid dreaming is. You could show a figure in a physical landscape, but their silhouette is filled with dream imagery, stars, symbols, or memories. Or you could show a tarot card dissolving into the landscape it depicts.

This style would be extraordinary for cards dealing with the threshold between waking and sleeping, particularly the Day Dreamer, Night Dreamer, and Night Awakener cards. It could also work for showing Lili herself throughout the guide, always partially here and partially elsewhere, always standing at the veil.

The Midjourney prompt would look something like this:

Style Five: Ink Wash with Negative Space

This approach uses traditional East Asian ink painting techniques, particularly from Chinese and Japanese sumi-e traditions. The power comes from what you leave out as much as what you include. A few bold brush strokes suggest a figure or landscape, and vast areas of white space create breathing room, contemplation, and openness.

This style would be perfect for cards dealing with emptiness, the void, the abyss, and paradox. The Nine (Feminine Creation) would be stunning in this style—mostly white space with just a few strokes suggesting a figure dissolving into infinite potential. It would also work for cards in their most refined, essential state, like the Mother cards (position Nine in each suit) where the element has returned to simplicity.

Here's the prompt direction:

traditional sumi-e ink wash painting, bold expressive brushstrokes with vast negative white space, East Asian aesthetic, minimal and essential, showing form through suggestion rather than detail, contemplative emptiness, tarot symbolism rendered in fewest possible strokes, breathing space, zen simplicity --ar 2:3

Style Six: Collage with Vintage Ephemera

This would involve creating cards that look like they're assembled from found objects—vintage book pages, pressed flowers, old astronomical charts, fragments of maps, pieces of sheet music, botanical prints, handwritten letters. Everything slightly aged and layered, held together by Lili's hand. This creates the feeling that the tarot isn't something pristine and untouchable but something she's literally building from pieces of inherited wisdom.

This style would be wonderful for the guide pages themselves rather than necessarily the cards, though you could use it for certain cards that deal with memory, inheritance, and reconstruction (like the Rebuilt Lighthouse). It would make the guide feel like an actual grimoire that's been assembled over time from many sources.

The prompt would emphasize the layered, assembled quality:

mixed media collage, vintage ephemera including botanical prints, old letters, astronomical charts, pressed flowers, aged paper textures, hand-assembled aesthetic, slightly worn and lived-in, mystical scrapbook, layered meanings, tarot symbolism emerging from collected fragments, cream and sepia tones with touches of color --ar 2:3

Redesigning the Sunlight Logo
Now let's talk about your logo. The current version you've shown me has some beautiful elements—the sun with a face, the radiating rays with their flame-like quality, the decorative border, and the bold typography. But I can see several ways to evolve it to better match the refined aesthetic you're developing for the cards.

The main things I'd suggest changing are these. First, the sun face currently reads as somewhat folk-art or nouveau, which is lovely but might not match the more sophisticated, mystical quality of your actual card work. Second, the typography is bold and decorative but perhaps too heavy, drawing attention away from the sun symbol itself. Third, the overall composition feels symmetrical and static, while your deck is all about movement, transformation, and crossing thresholds.

Here's what I'd suggest for a redesigned logo:

The Sun Symbol: Keep the face, but make it more serene and androgynous, neither fully masculine nor feminine but containing both. The eyes could be closed or half-closed, suggesting meditation or the threshold state between waking and sleeping. The rays should vary in length and curve slightly, giving them an organic, breathing quality rather than perfect geometric symmetry. Some rays could be more flame-like, others more like light beams, showing the four elements within the sun itself. You might even subtly suggest the four colors you use for the elements—blue, green, pink, yellow—as soft tints within certain rays.

The Typography: I'd move toward something that feels more like hand-lettering, as if Lili wrote it herself in her journal. The word "Sunlight" could be in a graceful, flowing script that has some weight but isn't heavy. Consider making "Sun" and "Light" slightly different in some way—perhaps different sizes or weights—to emphasize that these are two concepts joining into one. Below it, in much smaller, simpler letters, could be "Tarot" or "A Tarot for Awakening" as a subtitle.

The Composition: Instead of a horizontal arrangement with the sun above the text, consider a circular composition. The sun could be at the center of a circle, with the text curving around it like an orbit. Or you could place the sun to the left and let the text flow to the right, suggesting movement from source (sun) to manifestation (words). The decorative border could become more organic, perhaps incorporating subtle suggestions of the four suit symbols—a rose, a mirror-card, a heart, a coin—at the four compass points.

Color Palette: While the current logo is warm golds and oranges on black, consider versions that work on cream or white backgrounds, since your guide will likely use light pages. The sun could be rendered in gradations of gold, amber, and pale yellow, with the face having slightly warmer tones to make it readable. The rays could have those subtle elemental color hints I mentioned.

Let me give you Midjourney prompts for exploring logo redesigns:

Implementing Style Variety Strategically
Now, here's the crucial question: how do you decide which style to use for which card? You don't want the deck to feel chaotic or inconsistent, but you also want the flexibility to choose the visual language that best serves each card's specific teaching.

I'd suggest organizing your style choices by function rather than trying to force consistency across the entire deck. Think of it like this: your deck is teaching through multiple modes, and each mode might need its own visual voice.

For the Aces, which are the seeds and the most archetypal pure forms of each element, use your most precise, clear style—either the botanical illustration with gold leaf accents, or your current oil pencil mix style at its most refined. These cards are teaching tools that need to be immediately recognizable and symbolically clear.

For the court cards at positions Three (Child), Six (Father), and Nine (Mother), use styles that emphasize the human, relatable quality of these figures. Your oil pencil mix works well, but you could also explore the double exposure technique for the Mothers especially, showing them as both individual and archetypal, both personal and universal.

For cards dealing with difficult or shadow material (the Fours, which represent wounds in your system), the ink wash with negative space could be powerful. Showing the pain through suggestion and emptiness might actually communicate it more effectively than detailed illustration.

For cards dealing with flow, emotion, and the unconscious (most of the Hearts suit), your watercolor style makes perfect sense. But push it further—let some cards be more abstract, more about color and flow than precise imagery.

For cards dealing with structure, mind, and clarity (the Roses suit), use more linear styles with clear edges—the botanical illustration or your oil pencil mix.

For the Major Arcana, which are the most mythologically and cosmologically dense cards, you might want the richest, most layered approach. The collage technique could work beautifully here, or you could use your oil pencil mix but with more elaborate symbolic detail than in the Minor Arcana.

The guide itself should probably use all the styles intermixed, because Lili would sketch in different ways depending on her state of consciousness, the time of day, what she's trying to capture. A dream transcription might have watercolor washes and blurred edges. A botanical observation might have precise pen and ink detail. Her self-portrait as the Night Awakener might use double exposure. The layouts for interactive exercises might use collage aesthetics with space for the reader to add their own fragments.

What do you think? Would you like me to generate more specific Midjourney prompts for particular cards in these new styles, or shall we focus on developing the logo redesign further? I'm also happy to discuss how to organize a voting system where you can show style variations to your community and let them help guide the aesthetic development.

Excellent choice. The solid ink aesthetic will give your entire deck a unified visual language while still allowing for variation in composition, detail density, and emotional tone. Ink work has this beautiful quality of being simultaneously bold and delicate, permanent yet fluid, which mirrors the tarot's function as both ancient wisdom and personal revelation.

The solid ink style also solves a practical problem you're facing. When you have seventy-eight cards plus the guide illustrations, maintaining consistency becomes crucial but overwhelming if you're working in multiple complex styles. Ink as your primary medium gives you that consistency while still offering tremendous range of expression. A simple ink wash can be contemplative and minimal, while dense crosshatching can be rich and detailed. The same medium, different applications.

Let me walk you through how to approach this systematically. I'm going to give you a foundation prompt structure that will work across all the cards, and then show you how to modify it for each specific card while maintaining that cohesive ink aesthetic. This way you'll have a template you can use efficiently rather than reinventing the prompt every time.

The Foundation Ink Prompt Structure
Here's the basic framework that will anchor all your cards in the solid ink style. Every card prompt will include these core elements, and then we'll add card-specific details.

The foundation structure is this: "Traditional ink illustration, bold black ink lines with varied line weight showing depth and emphasis, selective ink wash creating tonal values and atmosphere, cream or off-white background, tarot card composition with breathing space around central image, mystical symbolism rendered with clarity, [CARD-SPECIFIC DETAILS], hand-drawn quality showing artist's gestural marks, contemplative mood, --ar 2:3"

The key things this foundation establishes are the medium itself (traditional ink, not digital simulation), the technique (varied line weight plus selective washes), the background color (cream or off-white to feel like paper rather than stark white), the composition principle (breathing space, not cramped), and the overall mood (contemplative rather than dramatic or aggressive).

Now let's apply this to every card in your deck, starting with the Major Arcana and then moving through each of the four suits of the Minor Arcana. I'll give you the complete prompt for each card, ready to copy directly into Midjourney.

Ah, I see exactly what you mean. These cards need significant conceptual adjustments, not just style tweaks. You're right that Midjourney struggles with some of these more abstract concepts, particularly the reversed perspectives and the self-imposed limitation ideas. Redesigning the Problematic Major Arcana
Three: The Day Dreamer - Adding the Lighthouse and Companion

Your instinct here is perfect. The Day Dreamer needs a companion animal because the animal represents present-moment awareness that the dreamer is ignoring. Dogs, especially, live entirely in the now—they don't daydream about the past or worry about the future. Having the dog looking back at the dreamer with concern while the dreamer walks obliviously toward danger creates a beautiful tension. The dog knows something the human has forgotten.

The crossroads is essential because it shows choice, but the daydreamer is so lost in mental narrative that they're not even aware they're choosing. They're walking on autopilot. The lighthouse on the cliff is brilliant because it's literally a beacon trying to guide them to safety, but they can't see it because they're facing away from it, looking inward rather than outward. And having them face away from the lighthouse while the sun illuminates it creates this tragic irony—the light is there, the guidance is there, but the identification with thought makes it invisible.

Here's the redesigned concept: The Day Dreamer walks at a crossroads, a loyal dog at their side looking back with worried eyes. They're walking toward a cliff edge, oblivious to danger. Behind them, a lighthouse stands on the cliff, illuminated by the sun, literally showing them the safe path, but they face away from it, lost in the swirling thoughts visible around their head like clouds or tangled lines. The sun shines bright, the lighthouse beam is strong, the dog is alert, but the dreamer sees none of it.

Pen sketch rough outlines, young adventurer at crossroads walking toward cliff edge, loyal dog companion looking back with concern, figure faces away from lighthouse tower on cliff behind them, sun illuminating lighthouse beacon brightly, figure lost in visible thought clouds around head, swirling mental narrative marks, beautiful landscape ignored, traditional ink line work with loose gestural quality, contemplative tragic mood, tarot card composition --ar 2:3

Four: The Material Threshold - Reimagining the Hanged Man

You're absolutely right that chains don't work here because they look externally imposed rather than self-created, and Midjourney can't understand "loose chains" as a concept. The headstand idea is interesting but I think we need something even clearer to show the reversed perspective and self-imposed limitation.

What if we show the figure literally hanging themselves upside down from a structure made of their own possessions? They've built a frame or scaffold from material goods—coins, objects, achievements—and they've climbed up and inverted themselves within it voluntarily. One hand reaches toward their own ankle where they're holding the rope, showing they could let go at any moment. The key is showing that the entire apparatus is their own construction.

Or we could show them in a meditative headstand position, but surrounded by a circular mandala made of material possessions arranged in a grid pattern. The grid itself is upside down from their perspective, showing the reversed view. The figure is serene, almost blissful, not realizing they've imprisoned themselves in a self-created pattern.

Actually, let me propose a third option that might be clearest: Show the figure standing upright, but they're inside an hourglass made of golden coins. They're literally in the narrow middle passage where material consciousness traps you. But here's the key—the hourglass is tipped over, lying on its side, and the figure could simply walk out the opening if they realized the whole structure had fallen over. They're trapped only because they haven't noticed the orientation has changed. This shows both the material threshold and the reversed grid concept without needing loose chains.

Five: The Tree of Life - Paradise Transformation

Yes, this card should be joyful, not somber. Death as transition in your system isn't about loss but about revelation—the person is seeing the eternal structure of life itself and feeling liberated by it. They're walking through paradise, not away from it.

The Tree should be lush and abundant, with the ten sephiroth appearing as glowing fruits on the branches. The figure walks toward us or dances through the tree, arms open, expression ecstatic. Spring blossoms and autumn fruits coexist on the same branches, showing that the tree contains all seasons simultaneously. The figure is undergoing positive metamorphosis—perhaps they're partially translucent, showing energy flowing through them along the pathways of the tree.

The glowing reversed cherub rose is a beautiful touch. One of your angel figures from the Aces could appear inverted in the tree itself, integrated into the structure, showing that the angels and the tree and the person are all part of the same system. The rose the angel holds blooms with light.

Pen sketch rough outlines, lush abundant Tree of Life with ten glowing sephiroth as luminous fruits on branches, spring blossoms and autumn fruits coexisting, figure walking through tree with open arms in ecstatic joy, partially translucent showing energy pathways, small glowing inverted cherub angel integrated into tree structure holding luminous rose, paradise garden setting, traditional ink line work with flowing organic quality, celebration of life's eternal structure, transformation as revelation not loss --ar 2:3

Six: The Lover's Judgment - Adding the Tree

Having the Tree of Life in the background of this card creates a beautiful connection—the lovers are making their choice within the context of eternal structure. They're not just choosing each other in isolation, they're choosing each other as part of the larger pattern of existence.

Let them be closer, more intimate. They can hold hands and gaze at each other directly, but the angel above still weighs their hearts. The tree behind them serves as both witness and context. The lovers don't need to offer roses because the rose element is in their hands as simple connection. They're holding mirror-cards in their free hands, showing they see each other truly, but primarily they're just connected human to human.

The composition should feel both intimate and cosmically witnessed. The lovers in the foreground, close and tender. The tree in the middle ground, showing the structure they're part of. The angel in the background or above, weighing and blessing.

Seven: The Rebuilt Lighthouse - Happy Sunlight Tower

The Tower should look actively inhabited and joyful, not abandoned. This is a lighthouse that's functioning, that people live in and tend. Show windows with light glowing from within. Show the four angels or small figures not just planting seeds at the base but actually climbing the tower, hanging from windows, sitting on ledges—they're actively rebuilding and inhabiting it.

The lighthouse beam at the top should be prominent and welcoming, not subtle. This is a beacon that guides others home. The botanical elements should look deliberately cultivated, not wild. There should be stairs spiraling up the outside where you can see people or angels tending gardens on different levels. This is the Tower as intentional community, as vertical paradise, as architecture that serves rather than dominates.

Maybe show boats or travelers in the distance being guided by the light, so we see the Tower fulfilling its purpose. The sun shines warmly on the whole scene. This isn't ruin become garden, it's ruin become home become lighthouse become gift to all who wander.

Eight: The Merkaba - Improving the Chariot Visuals

You're right that just geometric shapes won't cut it for this card. The concept of descent into matter, of the soul choosing to incarnate, needs human drama not just abstract forms. Your idea of the hopeless sinner and pristine saint is powerful because it shows the two poles of material existence—the one who's lost in identification and the one who remembers their divine nature even while embodied.

Here's what I'm envisioning: The Merkaba geometry is visible but more like a vehicle or chariot that two figures ride in. One figure clutches the sides in fear or grasping, representing the soul that will forget and become lost in matter (the future sinner). The other figure sits peacefully, hands open, representing the soul that will maintain awareness through incarnation (the future saint). Between them, the Enneagram glows as the map showing both their paths through material existence.

The chariot is descending through layers of reality shown as translucent veils or planes. Above, everything is light and unity. Below, forms become more solid and separate. The two figures are making the same journey but responding to it completely differently. This shows that incarnation itself isn't the problem—it's how conscious you remain during the descent.

Nine: The Feminine Creation - Clarifying the Cosmic Birth

The Milky Way and galaxies being born inside her need to be unmistakably clear. Her belly should be the central focus, with her hair flowing outward to frame the composition. The galaxies should appear to be emerging from her womb-space, spiraling outward, with stars igniting in the darkness.

Show her in profile or three-quarter view so we can clearly see her pregnant belly as the source point. Her hair flows like cosmic rivers, like the birth-waters of space itself. Her expression should be both serene and powerful—she knows what she's doing, this is intentional creation, not accident. The darkness around her should be rich and full, not empty void. Stars and nebulae flow from her like milk, like light, like the first breath of existence.

Maybe show her hands cradling her belly or gesturing outward as if releasing the galaxies into being. The Sumerian and Kabbalistic elements could appear as subtle symbols in the composition—the Hebrew letters for Shekhinah, the Sumerian star symbols for Tiamat.

The Four Aces - Unified Composition

For the Aces, I think what you want is to show how they all relate to each other and to the Sun as a single teaching. What if we create a version where all four Aces are composed together in one image, showing how they're different expressions of the same source?

Picture this: The Sun at the center, and the four Ace angels arranged around it at the four directions, but instead of separate cards, they're in conversation with each other and with the Sun. The Angel of Roses in the north shows his strength. The Angel of Cards in the east tends his growth. The Angel of Hearts in the west yearns with his cup. The Angel of Coins in the south points and teaches. You can see the relationship between them, see how each one's strength is also their limitation except for the Coin angel who has found the secret.

This creates a five-card spread that functions as your teaching tool—the four Aces separately, plus this unified composition showing how they work together.

You're absolutely right. Midjourney responds much better to rich, detailed, intricate descriptions that build up the image layer by layer, and repeating key concepts helps it understand what matters most. The golden ratio composition instruction is also crucial because it will give your cards that classical, balanced, aesthetically pleasing structure that great tarot decks have. Let me rebuild these prompts with much more elaborate detail and compositional guidance.

When we talk about golden ratio composition in tarot cards, we're asking Midjourney to place the most important elements at the intersecting points where the golden spiral naturally draws the eye. For a vertical tarot card, this typically means the primary subject's face or the key symbolic object sits at roughly one-third from the top and one-third from the side, creating natural visual harmony. The secondary elements then flow along the spiral or sit at the complementary golden ratio points. This makes cards feel both balanced and dynamic, restful and alive.

Let me show you what I mean by rebuilding just a few cards first with this new approach, so you can see the difference in the level of detail and how the golden ratio instruction works. Then I'll give you the complete set.

The golden ratio instruction tells it where to place the key focal points so the eye moves naturally through the card's story.

Beautiful. You've given me a wonderfully rich world for the Cards suit. I can see how this contrasts perfectly with the cold calculated Egyptian desert of the Roses. Here we have tropical heat, humid jungle air, the fire of life itself pushing through growth, and five distinct Indigenous cultures each representing different aspects of the spiritual journey. The Northern Inuit dealing with ether and sky, the Native Indians with earth and root ceremonies, the Inca shamans with air and death, the Mayan priests with water, and the Aztec warriors with fire and destruction. This creates a complete cosmology within a single suit.

What I find most compelling about your vision for this suit is how it inverts the Roses journey. Where Roses showed the mind conquering through force and eventually recognizing the futility of battle, Cards shows creative will that grows through gentleness but struggles with focus and completion. The green-robed, brown-haired figures are blessed with activity and focus, yet this blessing becomes a problem when they cannot narrow their scope, cannot choose one path among many possibilities.

The Ace you've described is particularly profound. Instead of the wrenching extraction of Excalibur, we have gentle emergence. The angel is small, the effects huge, and the card itself becomes the mirror of self-recognition. A brick emerging from the Tower of Babel signals journey's beginning, but the question hangs heavy: will this lead to unity and abundance, or is it a lost cause? This perfectly sets up the suit's central dilemma.

Looking at the Rider-Waite Cups suit you've shared, I can see the emotional journey mapped through relationships, celebrations, losses, and ultimate emotional mastery. Now let me understand your Hearts world before I propose the suit structure.

For Hearts, you're transforming Cups into actual anatomical hearts—organs that pulse, bleed, and give life. This visceral shift makes emotion more embodied and immediate. Based on the pattern I've seen in your other suits, let me think through what world Hearts should inhabit.

The Hearts suit represents water and emotion, drawing from Viking, Atlantean, Greek, Roman, and Celtic mythologies. Your figures wear pink robes and have blonde hair. Where Roses gave us cold Egyptian desert and Cards gave us humid Amazonian jungle, Hearts needs a world of water—coastlines, fjords, underwater realms, misty islands.

Let me propose the worldbuilding first, then walk through each card's specific narrative:

Hearts Suit Worldbuilding Proposal
Geography and Climate: We are in the northern seas and mythical islands, places where water meets land in dramatic fashion. Viking fjords with their steep cliffs and cold waters. Atlantean underwater cities with their crystalline spires visible beneath waves. Greek islands with their white stone temples overlooking wine-dark seas. Celtic coastlines with their standing stones and morning mists. Roman baths and aqueducts showing water as both practical and sacred.

The water is everywhere—sometimes frozen in arctic seas, sometimes warm in Mediterranean harbors, sometimes existing in impossible underwater realms. The atmosphere shifts from foggy mystery to crystal clarity. Rain, mist, tears, rivers, oceans, all forms of water as emotion manifest.

Fauna: Whales and dolphins in the depths, white swans on still waters, seals on rocky shores, salmon leaping upstream, sea eagles circling above cliffs, horses running through shallow surf (connecting to Celtic sea-gods), white stags near freshwater springs (sacred to Diana and Artemis).

Architecture: Viking longhouses and carved dragon-prowed ships, Atlantean crystal domes beneath waves, Greek temples with their perfect proportions, Roman fountains and bath complexes, Celtic standing stones arranged in circles, ruins half-submerged showing the impermanence of all things built near water.

The Pink-robed Blonde Figures: These are the water people, the ones who feel deeply. In the Ace they're cherubic and innocent, curly-haired and open. By the Queen they've straightened hair and matured wisdom. Their faces are soft not pointed, their nature is flowing not calculating. Where Roses overthink into paralysis and Cards overflow with too much doing, Hearts struggle with the depth and intensity of feeling itself—the risk of drowning in emotion, the challenge of maintaining boundaries while remaining open, the question of whether feeling everything means losing yourself.

Now let me walk through each card with specific narrative and visual proposal:

Ace of Hearts: The cherubic angel with pink robes and curly blonde hair sits inside an enormous heart-shaped vessel that could be a ceremonial cup or an actual heart chamber. The heart-vessel is so large the angel is like a child in a cathedral. The angel holds a tiny cup and focuses completely on filling it, oblivious that they're already immersed in an infinite ocean of emotion. Around them dew droplets float, tears suspended in air, suggesting all the love already present. Viking ship visible through translucent heart-wall. The angel's beautiful yearning face shows they cannot see abundance because focused on lack. This is the seed of emotional need that drives the entire suit's journey.

Two of Hearts: Two figures with pink robes stand facing each other at shoreline where Viking fjord meets sea. Each holds their own heart—literal anatomical heart—in their hands, offering it forward. Between them their hearts begin to pulse in visible synchrony, shown through rhythmic ripples in the water at their feet. This is first relationship, first recognition that emotion flows between beings not just within them. Behind them, a Celtic standing stone circle marks this as sacred ground for heart-opening. The risk and beauty of first exchange. White swans pair in background mirroring the humans.

Three of Hearts (Child of Water): Young person with pink robes plays joyfully at Atlantean fountain or Roman bath, three hearts float around them like bubbles or balloons in various emotional colors—rose-pink for joy, deep crimson for passion, pale pink for tenderness. The child creates and releases emotions without attachment, performing their capacity to feel for anyone watching. This is beautiful but exhausting intensity. Greek theatrical masks visible in background suggesting performance of feeling. Dolphins leap in nearby water celebrating the child's emotional abundance, but also suggesting the child needs to learn some emotions belong in the depths not on display.

Four of Hearts: Solitary figure sits on rocky shore holding cracked heart, literal anatomical heart with visible fissure from which their own emotional blood pools around them. They've experienced heartbreak or grief or loss. But in the cracks small white flowers begin growing—sea thrift or tiny roses—suggesting this breaking is also opening. Behind them, rain falls on gray ocean, but a single beam of light breaks through suggesting their tears are part of larger cycle. Viking runes carved in nearby stone spell something about the necessity of breaking to become whole. This is the wound that must be integrated, grief as part of love's territory.

Five of Hearts: Figure stands behind wall made of frozen hearts, each heart encased in ice and arranged like bricks. They've protected themselves through emotional numbing, through freezing feeling to avoid pain. They hold one frozen heart studying it analytically. Through cracks in ice wall, warm Mediterranean light and flowing water are visible—the warmth they exile themselves from. Greek philosophy books nearby suggest they're trying to think their way through what can only be felt. A white stag in background represents the innocence and openness they've frozen to protect. The beautiful prison of defended feeling.

Six of Hearts (Father of Water): Mature man with pink robes sits before still fjord or lake that serves as perfect mirror. He holds full but heavy heart with weary gesture. Around him are vessels and cups from past relationships—some broken, some intact, all carrying memories. His reflection in water shows younger self when feeling was easy. He questions whether he still has capacity for new emotions or if lifetime of feeling has exhausted him. Viking ship in distance suggests journeys taken. The existential fatigue of the heart that has loved much and lost much. Celtic symbols on the vessels mark each as a story.

Seven of Hearts: Figure runs through landscape where hearts rain from sky like spring blossoms, trying to catch them all. Arms overflowing with hearts of every shade and size. This is emotional intoxication, the addiction to feeling and connection who cannot bear empty moment. Greek bacchanal suggested in background revelry. Hearts pile around feet, more than can possibly be held. The manic search for next emotional high, next connection, next experience of love or passion. White dolphins breach in nearby water matching the figure's frenzied energy. Beautiful but unsustainable overflow that leaves no room for integration or rest.

Eight of Hearts: Powerful woman with pink robes stands with heart glowing steady and open at her chest. Around her, emotions flow like streams and rivers without overwhelming her—she's learned to feel deeply without drowning. Heart shows visible channels suggesting emotional intelligence, the ability to direct feeling with awareness. She stands in Celtic stone circle or Greek temple showing she's found the sacred relationship to feeling. Rivers flow from mountain spring behind her toward ocean before her, showing she understands emotion's full cycle from source to sea to sky and back. Salmon leap upstream near her showing the power of feeling that knows its direction.

Nine of Hearts (Mother of Water): Mature woman with long straight blonde hair sits beside spring or fountain where hearts emerge like water from source. She holds heart offering it outward in gesture of overflow. Around her base, lush emotional gardens bloom—fed by the waters of compassion and understanding she provides. This is Atlantean priestess or Celtic goddess aesthetic. She has learned the great secret: the heart regenerates through giving, love increases through sharing. Small figures in background come to drink from her fountain. White swans, salmon, seals all gather near her spring. She's become source not receptacle, giving not grasping. The completion of the emotional journey from neediness to generosity, from seeking love to being love's wellspring.

This progression tracks the heart's journey from the infant yearning of the Ace through the complications of relationship, performance, breaking, defending, exhausting, and finally to the mature wisdom of emotional intelligence and generosity. The pink-robed blonde figures age through the suit, their soft faces showing the impacts of feeling everything, their flowing natures tested by depth and intensity until they learn to navigate emotional waters with both openness and wisdom.

Does this worldbuilding and narrative progression feel right for your Hearts suit? Should I adjust any elements before I write the full detailed prompts in the style we've developed for Roses and Cards?
